- **question**: Should the feature's internal-failure channel be a runtime switch rather than a compile-time one, and should a health signal be added so that a dead logging subsystem is detectable? | **whyItMatters**: All four internal-failure reports and both echo paths write to a sink that is removed at compile time in every build lacking the diagnostic-build symbol, and no configuration in the source defines that symbol outside stock Debug. In shipped builds a completely dead logging subsystem - arming failed, every flush failing - is indistinguishable from a healthy one on every channel. The dossier explicitly recommends a clone add an out-of-band health signal. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:98,112,130,163,189; Directory.Build.props:1-5; all four project files (no compilation-symbol override anywhere).
- **question**: Which single per-user storage root convention should a clone adopt for all persisted state? | **whyItMatters**: The product writes logs to roaming application data, system prompts to local application data, and settings to the user-profile root - three roots for three kinds of state. On non-Windows hosts none of these locations is documented anywhere, and with no home directory set the log root degrades to a working-directory-relative path. A clone must pick one convention deliberately and document the non-Windows rendering. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:20; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/SystemPromptService.cs:15; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/SettingsService.cs:16; all five doc references render the log path as the Windows form only (docs/LLamaSharp-Token-Introspection.md:236,244; docs/LLamaSharp-Quick-Start.md:233; docs/LLamaSharp-Troubleshooting-0xC0000005.md:185,263,270).
- **question**: Should the process-global engine diagnostic destination be uninstalled at shutdown, and should a recorder reclaimed by automatic memory management perform a final flush? | **whyItMatters**: Shutdown does not uninstall the destination and the installed callback holds a reference to the buffer, so engine diagnostics keep accumulating into a buffer that will never be flushed, retaining memory for the process lifetime. Separately, the owning subsystem's finalizer skips recorder teardown entirely, so a subsystem collected rather than disposed loses its whole unflushed buffer silently. Both are latent today (one recorder per process) but become live the moment a clone creates more than one. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:81 vs :193-200; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/LLamaSharpService.cs:25,678-681,693-696; recorder construction sites src/ChatDbg/ChatShell.cs:33 and src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/Program.cs:26.
- **question**: Should entry timestamps be coordinated-universal with a recorded offset rather than bare local time rendered through the current culture? | **whyItMatters**: No timezone or offset is captured, so log lines are ambiguous across daylight-saving transitions and across machines - directly harming the support workflow this feature exists to serve. Compounding it, timestamps render through the current culture, and only the shipped publish configurations force invariant globalization, so the same source emits two different timestamp shapes depending on how it was built. No test covers either. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:83,121,149; src/ChatDbg/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Shell.csproj:52,95; searched the test file src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core.Tests/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfigTests.cs:10-153 - the timestamp format is unasserted.
- **question**: Should the size-triggered auto-flush also apply to application entries, given that they are the highest-volume producer? | **whyItMatters**: The threshold check exists only on the engine-diagnostic path. A generation with token-probability capture on emits one DEBUG entry per generated token on the hot loop, and none of them ever triggers a size-based flush - so the documented 'flushed every 10,000 characters' promise is only half true and the largest buffers are precisely the ones never trimmed. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:90 (present) vs :123-126 (absent); producer at src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/LLamaSharpService.cs:231; docs/LLamaSharp-Quick-Start.md:206.
- **question**: Should the shipped builds remain 64-bit-Windows-only, and should they keep stripping the exception detail these logs exist to capture? | **whyItMatters**: Nothing in this feature's own source is Windows-only - every primitive it uses is portable - yet both shells default the publish runtime identifier to 64-bit Windows while the assembly describes itself as cross-platform. Separately, the shipped configurations substitute bare resource keys for exception messages and strip symbols and stack-trace metadata, so in exactly the builds users run, every ERROR entry loses its content. These two decisions together determine whether the support workflow works at all for a shipped binary. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Shell.csproj:16,30,53,58,59,70,96; src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Shell.Gui.csproj:30,70; entry text at src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/LLamaSharpService.cs:100,107,571.
- **question**: Should the snapshot export signal success or failure to its caller, and should it refuse to write a 0-byte file? | **whyItMatters**: The export returns nothing and swallows every failure, so a caller cannot distinguish a successful capture from a permission-denied write from an empty buffer. Combined with the drain-on-flush behaviour, the ordinary post-generation export silently produces a zero-byte file that looks like a successful export. If the export-logs command is ever wired up, this is the first thing a user will hit. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:170-191 (unconditional write at :182, swallow at :187-190) vs the guarded daily flush at :154; re-exposed method at src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/LLamaSharpService.cs:653-656, which nothing in the repository calls.
- **question**: Is console-echo mode intended to ship at all? | **whyItMatters**: No code path in the product ever enables it; only tests and documentation examples mention it. When enabled it interleaves raw log lines directly into the user's chat transcript with no visual separation, which would be user-hostile. A clone should decide whether to keep the switch, and if kept, how to separate log output from chat output. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:35,101-104,133-136; searched all of src/ for any assignment setting it true - none found outside tests.
- **question**: How should a clone handle the possibility that the inference engine delivers partial diagnostic lines rather than whole ones? | **whyItMatters**: The source appends a line terminator per callback invocation, so a multi-part engine message would fragment across several independently-timestamped lines, corrupting the one-entry-per-line contract that makes the file readable by line-oriented tools. INFERRED - the callback path has no test coverage at all and the engine's own behaviour could not be read (the binding package is not vendored in the repository). | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:81-87; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core.Tests/Services/TokenInspection/LLamaSharpLogConfigTests.cs:10-153 (no test reaches the callback path); the severity enum's member names and numeric values could not be read - not vendored, not in a local package cache.
- **question**: Should the three display-mode settings (show-all-tokens, grid-layout, grid-maximum-alternatives) be restored at startup, or should the clone reproduce the source's silent omission so every session starts in sample/list mode? | **whyItMatters**: It is the difference between a setting that works and a setting that appears to save but never takes effect. - **question**: Should end of stream on standard input end the session, or reproduce the source's infinite prompt-reprinting spin? | **whyItMatters**: As shipped, piping input or closing standard input makes the process unkillable-by-itself and unusable in any script or CI context. - **question**: Should the leading-slash dispatch test be an ordinal byte comparison or the source's locale-aware prefix match? | **whyItMatters**: With the locale-aware match, an ignorable-character-prefixed line is dispatched as a command and then mis-parsed; worse, the same input dispatches differently between the default build and the size-optimised builds that enable invariant globalization. - **question**: Should system-prompt name lookup be case-sensitive, case-insensitive, or inherit the host file system's rules as the source does? | **whyItMatters**: Today the same settings file loads a different system prompt - and therefore produces different model behaviour - on different operating systems, with no diagnostic message at all. - **question**: Should the user's message be appended to the transcript before or after the provider lookup and configuration check? | **whyItMatters**: As shipped, failed and unconfigured turns leave orphan user messages that are silently re-sent as billed context on the next successful turn, so a user who mistypes a provider name pays for their mistakes later. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg/ChatShell.cs:346 vs :349,356. - **question**: Should all three provider adapters be constructed and disposed every session, or only the selected one? | **whyItMatters**: Eager construction opens an unused outbound connection pool and installs a native diagnostic buffer, and disposal writes a daily log file under roaming application data even in a session that never touched the local model. - **question**: Should the demonstration mode's fabricated values be corrected into log space (or bypass exponentiation), or reproduced as-is? | **whyItMatters**: The demonstration writes 70-98 into the log-probability field, so display code exponentiates it and shows e^70-scale 'probabilities' with every token in the top colour band. Verified by repository-wide symbol search accounting for the full-screen project's build file, which excludes its services folder and re-includes two files by name.
- **question**: Does the released bundled artefact actually run at all, given that the shipping profile suppresses the runtime-configuration side-car a bundled just-in-time artefact needs to start? | **whyItMatters**: If it does not, the product has never shipped a working binary and the entire distribution promise is unmet. It also determines whether the side-car suppression is a bug to fix or a setting the clone must reproduce. | **whereWeLooked**: Both executable project descriptors (the setting appears identically in both profiles); the embedded dependency manifest of the one committed pre-built artefact, which contains a dependency manifest but no runtime-options block; all four build scripts; the release pipeline; all packaging documents. Building and executing was out of scope, and the committed binary was deliberately not executed.
- **question**: What is the current artefact size once the two simultaneously-referenced native inference backends are included? | **whyItMatters**: The documented 13-25 MB budget is the only quantified quality bar this feature has, and two full native machine-learning backends make it implausible. Without a real number a reimplementer cannot size the download or judge whether the compression and trimming choices are worth their cost. | **whereWeLooked**: The only measured artefact in the repository predates the local-inference dependency by a week. The pipeline measures a size and writes it to a step output that nothing reads, so no size survives a run. No artefact built after the inference dependency was added exists anywhere.
- **question**: Was the release pipeline ever executed successfully after the framework version bump? | **whyItMatters**: The pipeline installs a major runtime line older than the one the projects target, so it should fail before compiling. If it has never run since the bump, every claim about the pipeline working is unverified and the clone should assume it needs repair, not translation. | **whereWeLooked**: No run logs, no tags and no release metadata are present in the repository. The last change to the pipeline predates the framework bump.
- **question**: Is the native ahead-of-time profile viable at all, given reflection-heavy provider client libraries, reflection-driven serialization at every persistence call site, and non-trimmable native inference payloads? | **whyItMatters**: It determines whether a reimplementation should offer a native profile at all, or ship only the bundled one. Offering an unbuildable profile costs documentation, scripts and menu options that mislead developers. | **whereWeLooked**: Trim and ahead-of-time analysis diagnostics are suppressed everywhere, so the source contains no evidence either way. No native artefact exists in the repository, and nothing in the pipeline ever exercises that profile.
- **question**: Is the native compiler and linker required by ahead-of-time compilation expected to be pre-installed, or should the build install it? | **whyItMatters**: Nothing in the source installs or even names it, while the stated prerequisites list only a managed toolchain, 8 GB of RAM and solid-state storage. A clone that copies the prerequisites verbatim inherits a profile that cannot build on a stock machine. | **whereWeLooked**: All four build scripts, every pipeline step, and the prerequisites section of the build documentation.
- **question**: How is the release publication credential provisioned, scoped and rotated? | **whyItMatters**: It is a long-lived personal access token held in a repository secret, used in the same job that interpolates operator-supplied text into shell commands. Its scope and rotation policy determine the blast radius of that exposure. | **whereWeLooked**: Only the secret's name is observable in the pipeline. No provisioning, scoping or rotation is documented anywhere, and the project documentation incorrectly claims no external secret is required.
- **question**: How are the copyleft licence's binary-distribution obligations intended to be met? | **whyItMatters**: The release attaches only executables, with no licence copy, notice file or source offer. A reimplementation must make a deliberate, documented decision rather than inheriting the omission. | **whereWeLooked**: The licence file, the release publication step's asset list, the generated release notes template, and all release documentation.
- **question**: Is the graphical terminal shell intended to ship, and if so which artefact is 'the product' for a user? | **whyItMatters**: The graphical shell carries complete, byte-identical distribution profiles but is referenced by no script and no pipeline step, while the user manual presents its interface as the primary one. A downloader currently receives a different program from the one documented. | **whereWeLooked**: All four build scripts, every pipeline step, both executable project descriptors, and the user manual. Intent is recorded nowhere.
- **question**: Should the two mutually exclusive native inference backends (processor and graphics-vendor compute) both ship in every artefact on every platform? | **whyItMatters**: They are the dominant size constraint and the reason the native profile cannot produce a single file. Troubleshooting documentation already proposes dropping one as a remedy, which makes the backend set a packaging decision the clone must make explicitly. | **whereWeLooked**: The shared library's dependency pins, the native-payload troubleshooting document, and both distribution profile blocks. No conditional or optional reference exists anywhere.
- **question**: Should the three scattered per-user state roots be consolidated, and what is the uninstall story? | **whyItMatters**: A packaged artefact writes settings, prompts and logs into three different per-user roots under two naming conventions, none of them documented and none of them removable by any product action. On a POSIX host they resolve to three unrelated locations. | **whereWeLooked**: The settings store, the prompt store and the inference log configuration; the user manual and all packaging documentation, none of which mentions any of the three.
- **question**: Should the settings split-brain be fixed, so commands and the window share one settings record? | **whyItMatters**: Today Change Model and the typed set/logprobs/prompt commands persist a record that still holds factory defaults, silently overwriting the user's saved provider, endpoint and model on disk while the status line and the model calls use a different record. This is data loss, and a clone that reproduces the wiring inherits it. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/Program.cs:14, :31-47, :58, :79-87; src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/ChatWindow.cs:1061-1078. No document or commit message explains the ordering.
- **question**: Should the demo visualisation be re-rendered in-window instead of written to the raw console stream? | **whyItMatters**: As shipped it produces no visible output, corrupts the screen on the next repaint, colours every token red and prints values 100x too small, yet still reports success. A reimplementation must decide whether the demo becomes a real in-window panel or is dropped. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/Program.cs:20, :41; src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/Services/SpectreConsoleFormatter.cs; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenFormatters.cs:36-55. Not observed at runtime - the environment's toolchain was broken.
- **question**: Is the probability scale 0-1 or 0-100, and which components must be corrected to agree? | **whyItMatters**: The window's panel and percent formatter assume 0-1 and are correct; the shared colour/format helpers and one unit test assume 0-100, and that test is arithmetically unsatisfiable so the suite is permanently red. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Models/TokenLogProbabilities.cs:26, :188; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core.Tests/Models/TokenLogProbabilityTests.cs:9-18; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/TokenFormatters.cs:36-55. Dossier guidance: pick 0-1, because that is what the shipping window renders.
- **question**: Should credential save and credential migration report their real outcome? | **whyItMatters**: Both report success unconditionally. On a host whose credential store is not implemented, or with an unrecognised credential type, the user is told the secret was saved while nothing was stored - a security-relevant false assurance. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/SettingsDialog.cs:543-545, :586-600; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/SettingsService.cs:147-151, :157-166, :200-203.
- **question**: Should the credential migration flow stop being an interactive plain-console wizard? | **whyItMatters**: It prints a numbered menu and blocks twice on standard input that the full-screen toolkit is also draining, so the application appears to hang. A clone must move it into a dialog or make migration non-interactive. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/SettingsDialog.cs:586-600 leading to src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/SettingsService.cs:196-268 (:210-211, :246-247).
- **question**: Should the settings storage layer stop swallowing its own write failures? | **whyItMatters**: Two error dialogs the code contains are unreachable, and a settings write into a read-only directory closes the dialog as if it had saved, so the user loses configuration silently. This also affects the console shell. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/SettingsService.cs:85-104; src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/SettingsDialog.cs:505; src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/SystemPromptsDialog.cs:224.
- **question**: Where should storage-layer diagnostics go while a full-screen interface owns the screen - a dialog, a log file, or nowhere? | **whyItMatters**: Every settings, prompt-parse and credential diagnostic is written straight to the console, painting garbage over the window; the user never learns the real reason anything failed. | **whereWeLooked**: src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/SettingsService.cs:69, :73, :80, :102, :110, :149, :185, :191, :205; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Services/SystemPromptService.cs:65, :92.
- **question**: Should destructive actions gain confirmations, and should the delete confirmation stop defaulting to Yes? | **whyItMatters**: Clear History and Import both destroy the whole conversation with no confirmation and no undo, while prompt deletion confirms but makes the destructive answer the default so Enter deletes. The two are inconsistent in opposite directions. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/ChatWindow.cs:269; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Commands/ImportCommand.cs:42-45; src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/SystemPromptsDialog.cs:442-446.
- **question**: Should file paths chosen in the pickers bypass the space-delimited command dispatcher? | **whyItMatters**: Picker-chosen paths are formatted into a command string, split on spaces with empties discarded, then re-joined with single spaces, so any path with two or more consecutive spaces is silently corrupted and the file is written to the wrong place. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/ChatWindow.cs:1014, :1031, :384; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Commands/ImportCommand.cs:28, :75; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Commands/ExportCommand.cs:28.
- **question**: Should blank lines inside a message be preserved in the transcript? | **whyItMatters**: Every whitespace-only wrapped line is dropped on render, so paragraph breaks, blank lines inside fenced code and deliberate spacing disappear; the on-screen text no longer matches what the model returned or what an export writes. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/ChatWindow.cs:543. No document mentions the behaviour.
- **question**: Should the wrap-width computation be guarded against a non-positive viewport? | **whyItMatters**: A transcript render with a non-empty conversation and a viewport of 4 columns or fewer asks for a negative-length substring and crashes the application unguarded; it is survivable at startup only because the conversation is empty then. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/ChatWindow.cs:507, :539, :749-789, first call at :213.
- **question**: Should transient-status timers be cancellable, and should the in-flight indicator survive a long request? | **whyItMatters**: Each status update starts its own uncancellable 3-second timer that restores the resting text regardless of what has been posted since, so the 'Thinking...' indicator vanishes mid-request and back-to-back messages wipe each other. | **whereWeLooked**: src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/UI/ChatWindow.cs:903-916, :849, :862, :900.

This is a substantive behavioural difference from the cloud back ends that no documentation mentions, and no code resets the engine session. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:131-136, :361-366, :606-610; Commands/ClearCommand.cs:18-23; Commands/ImportCommand.cs:43.
- **question**: Which context-size fallback is correct when the configured value is not positive — 2048 or 4096? | **whyItMatters**: The loader falls back to 2048 while the per-step state snapshot in the same run reports 4096, and the documented default is 4096. At least one is wrong, and the snapshot's 'remaining context' number is derived from it. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:550 versus :291-292; Models/ChatSettings.cs:54; Commands/SetCommand.cs:97-104.
- **question**: What should happen when the context window fills? | **whyItMatters**: Currently unhandled: no detection, no truncation, no eviction, no warning. The reimplementation needs an explicit policy (fail the turn, truncate the prompt, or slide the window), because a long local session will hit it. | **whereWeLooked**: Verified absent from LLamaSharpService.cs; the state snapshot's remainingContext at :287-294 gates nothing.
- **question**: Should context accounting be honest, or should the 'remaining context' number be dropped? | **whyItMatters**: Tokens-processed, context-token-count and remaining-context all count generated pieces only; the prompt, the system prompt and every retained prior turn contribute nothing, so remaining-context is wrong by the entire prompt length. Real accounting requires tokenising the prompt, which this feature deliberately avoids by delegating templating to the engine. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:287-294; docs/LLamaSharp-Quick-Start.md:66-86.
- **question**: Should failures keep masquerading as assistant replies? | **whyItMatters**: Every absorbed failure is returned as a success-shaped response whose text begins 'Error running local LLM: ', which both shells append to the transcript as an assistant turn and print like model output; the response's dedicated error field is populated and read by nobody. Exported transcripts therefore contain error strings indistinguishable from model output. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:98-110; src/ChatDbg/ChatShell.cs:377-381, :396-399.
- **question**: Should the stop sequences be configurable? | **whyItMatters**: Two hard-coded strings tied to one prompt convention truncate replies from models using a different chat template, and truncate any reply in which the model legitimately writes 'User:'. Nothing documents them. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:144, :374.
- **question**: Should the engine log hook be per instance or process-global, and must shutdown unregister it? | **whyItMatters**: The hook is global while the arm-once flag is per instance, so a second instance silently steals it; shutdown never unregisters it and the component keeps buffering and writing to disk after shutdown. The target platform's binding may only offer a global hook, in which case the arm-once flag and the disposal path must move to process scope. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpLogConfig.cs:15, :69-70, :81-105, :119-137, :142-165, :193-200.
- **question**: Should the adjacent tokenise and inspect commands share this feature's resident model? | **whyItMatters**: Today each loads its own separate copy of the same multi-gigabyte file at context sizes 512 and 2048 respectively and disposes it immediately, so a single tokenise request pays a full second load. Sharing requires a decision about who owns the cached model's lifetime. | **whereWeLooked**: Services/TokenInspectionService.cs:30-41, :104-118; Commands/TokenizeCommand.cs:35-61; Commands/InspectCommand.cs:35-77.
- **question**: Which inference-engine version and which host runtime should the port target, and should exactly one accelerator backend ship? | **whyItMatters**: The source pins a release-candidate runtime and engine version 0.25.0 while its own troubleshooting document names the previous runtime plus engine version 0.17.0 as the proven combination, and twice names shipping both backends together as a cause of native load failures — yet both are referenced unconditionally. A port should choose deliberately rather than inherit the pins. | **whereWeLooked**: global.json; src/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core/Xcaciv.ChatDbg.Core.csproj:4, :13-15; docs/LLamaSharp-Troubleshooting-0xC0000005.md:32-37, :93-109, :171-175, :233-245, :310-319.
- **question**: Should the three unwired diagnostic operations (analysis read-back, analysis export, log export) be wired to user-reachable commands, or removed? | **whyItMatters**: All three are implemented and working; none has a caller. The three matching commands exist, are described in help text and two guides, are placeholders returning a 'requires LLamaSharp provider integration' notice, and are registered in neither shell — so the notice cannot even be reached. A shipped test asserts the placeholder text, protecting the stub. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:628, :633-648, :653-656; Commands/{ShowTokenAnalysis,ExportTokenAnalysis,ExportLogs}Command.cs; registration verified absent from src/ChatDbg/ChatShell.cs:42-58 and src/ChatDbg.Shell.Gui/Program.cs:30-50.
- **question**: Should a generation be cancellable and should the process-wide locks have a timeout? | **whyItMatters**: There is no cancellation, no timeout, no queue bound and no progress feedback anywhere in this feature. A user cannot abort a long local generation, and a second concurrent request blocks silently and indefinitely. Shutdown is likewise unsynchronised against an in-flight generation, so native resources can be released underneath the streaming loop. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:23-24, :72, :113, :670-685; verified absent: any cancellation token in the product.
- **question**: Should the candidate computation use a partial selection instead of a full sort? | **whyItMatters**: The current routine converts the entire score vector to double precision, materialises the complete index range and fully sorts it descending once per generated piece over a 32 000–128 000 entry vocabulary. The source's own planning document prescribes quickselect. This is the dominant per-token cost of the introspection path. | **whereWeLooked**: LLamaSharpService.cs:459-463; docs/llamasharp-lowlevel-api-implementation-plan.md:89.

---

## Candidate product-wide rules

- Credential resolution chain (product-wide): every secret is resolved fresh on each read through a fixed priority chain — process environment variable, then an opt-in operating-system secret vault entry, then the value stored in the settings file. Failures at any leg are swallowed and the chain continues. Nothing is cached.
- Settings file path convention (product-wide): the settings record lives at <user profile directory>/.ChatDbg/settings.json, falling back to the system temporary directory when the user profile directory cannot be resolved.
- Environment-variable naming convention (product-wide): secrets are supplied through variables named CHATDBG_<PROVIDER>_<PURPOSE>, e.g. CHATDBG_AZURE_API_KEY.
- Secret vault entry naming convention (product-wide): vault entries are generic credentials named ChatDbg:<SettingName>, e.g. ChatDbg:AzureApiKey.
- Asynchrony convention (product-wide): all service and command entry points are asynchronous and none accept a cancellation signal; no in-flight operation can be aborted by the user.
- Error-message wrapping convention (product-wide): a backend re-raises internal failures with a provider-scoped prefix and the inner message appended; nested handlers append their own prefixes, so multi-level prefixes are the norm and shells add a final 'Error getting AI response: ' prefix.
- Validation-placement convention (product-wide, currently inconsistent): range and enumeration validation lives only in interactive command handlers; values loaded from disk are never validated. This should be settled once for the whole product.
- No caching convention (product-wide): no client caching, no response caching, no credential caching, no configuration snapshot. Every operation re-reads settings and re-resolves secrets.
- No retry, timeout, back-off, rate-limit handling, circuit breaker or audit log exists anywhere in the product; the only bound on any remote call is the transport's platform default.
- Access-control model (product-wide): the only access control is possession of the provider credential. There are no roles, no per-user authorisation, and no audit trail.
- Diagnostic-trace convention (product-wide): traces go to a developer-only sink never surfaced to the user, and may contain full prompts and conversations — they must be treated as sensitive in any deployment.
- Localisation convention (product-wide): all user-visible strings are fixed English literals; there are no resource files and no localisation hooks.
- Platform-coupling convention (product-wide): all logic is platform-neutral except the operating-system secret vault leg, which is Windows-only and silently yields nothing elsewhere. Any port needs a per-platform substitute or a documented environment-variable-only posture.
- Conversation-state convention (product-wide): the entire conversation is re-sent on every turn with no server-side thread, no delta, no history-window trimming and no client-side token accounting; the user message is appended to history before the send, so a failed turn leaves it in place.
- No authentication, authorisation, tenancy, role check, permission model, rate limiting or audit log exists anywhere in the product. Any user who can reach the prompt can invoke any registered capability; commands that touch credentials or the filesystem enforce their own internal rules only.
- Outcome-reporting convention: user-visible success is prefixed with U+2713 followed by a space, failure with U+2717 followed by a space. In the console host the glyph is the only machine-readable success/failure signal - there is no per-command exit code and no structured output. Any clone targeting assistive technology must add a second signal.
- Process exit-code convention: 0 on normal shutdown; 1 when a fault escapes the session, preceded by the printed line 'Fatal error: <message>'. These are the only machine-readable signals the whole application emits.
- Async convention: every service and command entry point is asynchronous, and no cancellation token exists anywhere in the product. There is no timeout, cancellation, progress indication or interrupt for any long-running operation on any path.
- Wiring convention: there is no dependency-resolution container anywhere. All collaborators are constructed by hand in a fixed literal order and passed positionally, and every shared state object (the conversation transcript, the configuration record) is mutated in place by whoever holds it. A host that builds collaborators before loading persisted state must reconnect them to the loaded state rather than replacing the variable.
- File-path convention: a leading '~/' is expanded to the user profile directory, but '~\\' (backslash form) is not, on every path that accepts a file path. Default file locations are under the user profile; the default exported transcript file name is chat_history.json.
- Localisation: none. Every name, section heading, dialog title, prompt and message in the product is hard-coded English, and no localisation hook exists.
- Globalisation: identifiers typed by the user are lower-cased with a culture-independent mapping before lookup so locale-specific casing rules cannot break matching; but the slash-prefix test and the alphabetical orderings use culture-sensitive comparisons, and two shipped packaging configurations enable invariant-globalisation mode, which changes them. All such comparisons should be ordinal.
- Observability: user actions are not logged anywhere. The only diagnostic output on the control path is full fault detail written to a debug trace channel by the console host; the user sees only the fault message.
- Encoding: all embedded user-visible text must live in one source encoding (UTF-8). Exactly one source file violates this and produces replacement characters in rendered help.
- Concurrency: the application is strictly serial - one user-initiated operation at a time, awaited to completion before the next input is accepted. There is no queueing, parallelism, locking or re-entrancy protection anywhere.
- Failure atomicity: partial mutations of shared state are never rolled back. There is no transaction, compensation or undo concept anywhere in the product.
- Documentation-versus-code precedence: README.md, IMPLEMENTATION_SUMMARY.md and both stale copies of the in-repo product brief disagree with the code in several places (command counts, provider counts, menu parity, target runtime version). Code is authoritative; documentation is a hint only.
- Provider registry convention: exactly three back ends exist, keyed by the lower-cased literals 'azure', 'bedrock', 'llama'; the configured provider value is lower-cased before lookup; any other value is rejected with the exact message "Provider must be 'azure', 'bedrock', or 'llama'"; an unknown key at turn time yields "Error: Unknown AI provider: <key>" in the console host and a modal titled 'Error' reading "Unknown AI provider: <key>" in the windowed host.
- Shared back-end contract: every chat back end must (1) report a display name, (2) answer a readiness question from a settings record with no side effects the caller must undo, (3) answer a conversation as plain text and never return nothing (a fallback sentence instead), (4) answer a conversation with an optional token-probability list, and (5) be disposable.
- Error convention: back ends communicate failure by raising an operation-invalid error carrying a human-readable message, never by populating the response envelope's error field; the console host renders it as 'Error getting AI response: <message>' and continues the session, while the windowed host shows a modal titled 'Error' with body 'Failed to get AI response: <message>'.
- Cancellation convention: no cancellation signal is threaded anywhere in the product; no in-flight operation can be aborted. This is a repo-wide convention, not a per-feature omission.
- Transport-policy convention: no back end configures a timeout, retry count, back-off, proxy or connection pooling; whatever the underlying client library defaults to is what runs.
- Settings file path convention: the settings document lives at <user profile>/.ChatDbg/settings.json, indented, with camel-cased member names, falling back to the system temporary directory when the user-profile folder is unavailable. System prompts live under local application data.
- Credential storage convention: secrets are never persisted in the settings document by any supported user action; resolution order is a ChatDbg-specific environment variable, then the standard vendor environment variable, then an optional operating-system vault entry named 'ChatDbg:<Name>', then a deprecated read-only settings-document member. First non-empty wins; all-empty yields the empty string.
- Credential vault convention: the operating-system vault is optional and capability-detected; every failure, including 'this platform has no vault', is swallowed and reported as 'not found' with no message. Remediation and help text must hide vault options the running platform cannot offer.
- Secret hygiene convention: credential values are never printed, never traced, and appear in the settings listing only as '***set***' or '(not set)' alongside a source label. (One exception exists in the credential feature's migration flow, which echoes legacy keys in plaintext.)
- Credential source labels are a fixed vocabulary shared across providers: 'environment variable (<VARIABLE NAME>)', 'Windows Credential Manager', 'settings file (deprecated)', 'not set'.
- Tuning-value ranges are product-wide, enforced by the settings command and clamped rather than rejected by the windowed dialog: temperature 0.0-2.0 inclusive (default 0.7), max output tokens 1-8192 inclusive (default 1000), top-K alternatives 1-20 inclusive (default 5), probabilities-enabled default false.
- Diagnostic trace convention: full request bodies, full reply bodies and full failure details are written to a developer-build-only trace channel with no redaction or truncation; credential values are excluded.
- Command surface convention: input beginning with '/' is a command and everything else is a chat turn; command-flagged history entries are excluded from every model request; the user's turn is appended to history before any readiness check, so failed turns leave orphan user messages.
- Internationalisation convention: all operator- and user-facing strings are English literals; role names, member names and family prefixes are compared case-insensitively / culture-invariantly, and numeric values are serialised culture-invariantly.
- Testability convention: each cloud back end exposes a substitutable single-operation client-construction seam so the whole build/invoke/parse path is exercisable with zero network; the product never registers an alternative implementation.
- Observability convention: there are no metrics, no captured request identifiers and no latency measurement; the response envelope's elapsed-time field is left at zero by the cloud back ends.
- Settings file location convention: the settings file lives at <user profile directory>/.ChatDbg/settings.json by default; if the resolved base directory is blank or resolving it raises, the base directory falls back to the operating system temp directory with the file name unchanged, silently and with no message. The base directory and file name are both overridable at construction of the settings store, and the store reports the resolved path (which the welcome banner prints).
- Settings persistence convention: the settings directory is created on demand at save time; the file is written pretty-printed with camelCase key naming and explicit per-field name overrides; computed and resolved members are excluded from serialization; every mutation rewrites the entire file rather than patching it.
- Settings load-failure convention: a load that raises prints 'Error loading settings: <message>' and returns a fully default record, silently discarding everything the file held, and the next save overwrites the unreadable file.
- Settings save-failure convention: a save that raises prints 'Error saving settings: <message>' and then returns normally with no failure signal, so every caller in the product reports success while nothing reached disk. This affects every feature that persists a setting.
- Command result convention: every command returns a success/failure outcome with an optional message; commands that delegate to a service which already persisted state suppress the generic save-after-change step, while an ordinary setting change performs exactly one save.
- Command error convention: any unhandled fault inside a command is caught and returned as 'Error setting <key>: <message>' rather than propagating.
- Command argument convention: the command key is lower-cased before dispatch; the command line is tokenized by splitting on the single space character with empty tokens discarded, so runs of spaces collapse to one and tabs and newlines cannot be entered at all. This governs every command's argument handling, not just credentials.
- Authentication and permission model: there is no authentication, no authorization, no administrator role, no multi-user model, and no server side. Everything is single-user, single-process, local. The only permission model is the operating system's own; the product performs no permission check, applies no file mode or access-control-list, and nothing it does requires elevated rights.
- Audit and logging convention: there is no structured logging, no log sink, and no audit record of any operation anywhere in this feature. The only trace is a line on the console. Nothing records when a secret was stored, rotated, or read.
- Concurrency convention: single-threaded interactive assumptions throughout. No locking, no file lock, and no atomic rename around settings load or save; a whole-file read-modify-write loses a concurrent edit outright, and two instances sharing one settings file interleave writes with no detection. Interactive prompts block the calling thread on standard input with no timeout and no cancellation signal.
- Interaction-port convention (recommended, currently absent): the service layer writes prompts to standard output and reads from standard input directly, which makes those flows unusable under a full-screen terminal interface and untestable without a real console. Any surface that prompts should go through an injected interaction port.
- Localization convention: all user-visible text is a hard-coded single-language literal. There is no message catalogue, no formatting indirection, and no locale awareness anywhere in the product.
- Source-encoding convention: source files must be stored as UTF-8 and must emit real characters or plain ASCII markers. The source contains two distinct text-corruption defects (question-mark glyphs committed as literals in one file, and a raw 0x95 Windows-1252 bullet byte in others) that corrupt user-visible output across multiple features.
- Documentation-versus-code convention: where the repository's documentation and the code disagree, the code is the specification. Several documents in the source describe aspirational or partially-complete work.
- Single-user, no-auth model: no authentication, no authorization, no accounts and no per-user data separation anywhere in the product. Whoever runs the process owns all in-memory and on-disk state.
- Command contract: every user-facing command exposes a name (the dispatch key), a one-line description and a usage string, accepts an ordered list of argument strings, and yields a result carrying a success flag, an optional user-facing message and an exit-requested flag.
- Argument tokenization: both shells split the typed line on single spaces and discard empty tokens. There is no quoting mechanism, so runs of consecutive spaces in any argument (message text, file path) collapse to one wherever a command re-joins its arguments.
- Command names are matched case-insensitively (lower-cased before lookup); arguments are not lower-cased by the dispatcher.
- Command routing: a line whose first character is '/' is always a command in both shells and never becomes chat content. There is no escape sequence for sending a literal leading slash to the model.
- Result decoration: the plain-console shell prefixes a successful command's message with a check glyph and a failed one with a cross glyph; the full-screen shell shows the check-prefixed message in the status bar and raises a modal box titled 'Command Error' on failure, or 'Error' for an unhandled failure.
- Status-bar messages in the full-screen shell revert to the default provider/model line after 3000 ms, via an unconditional timer started for every message.
- Error-reporting convention (defect-shaped, product-wide): service-layer failures are swallowed, their real cause written to the standard output stream, and reduced to a boolean; the user-facing message names the operation and the path but never the cause. Because stdout writes bypass the terminal interface, this also corrupts the full-screen layout.
- File-path convention: a leading '~/' — exactly those two characters at position 0 — expands to the current user's home/profile directory. A bare '~', a backslash form, and '~someuser/' are not expanded.
- No path sandboxing anywhere: no canonicalisation, no allow-list, no root restriction. Any path the operating system permits is read or written verbatim.
- No cancellation: no operation anywhere accepts a cancellation signal.
- No localization: every user-visible message, usage string and error is a hard-coded English literal. Non-ASCII decoration glyphs require a Unicode-capable terminal font.
- Unhandled-failure convention: the plain-console shell catches per-loop-iteration, prints 'Error: {message}' and continues; the full-screen shell shows a modal error box and continues.
- No audit trail, no telemetry and no structured logging of user actions in this feature.
- Configuration file path convention: the product's own state lives under a literal '.ChatDbg' directory inside the user's profile directory, used unchanged on every operating system, with the OS temporary directory as the degraded fallback when the profile directory is null, empty, whitespace, or throws. (Settings use <user profile>/.ChatDbg/settings.json; system prompts use a sibling location under local app data.)
- Command result convention: every user-facing command returns a result carrying a success flag, an optional message, and an exit-requested flag, produced by success/error/exit factories. Validation failures return an error result carrying a specific human-readable sentence; nothing is mutated and nothing is persisted on a failed validation.
- Error-message convention: caught exceptions are surfaced as '<Operation context>: <platform exception message>' (Error loading settings: ..., Error saving settings: ..., Error setting <key>: ..., Error configuring log probabilities: ..., Error during migration: ..., Failed to save settings: ...). Because the shipped compact/single-file packaging strips framework resource strings, a reimplementation should carry its own diagnostic text instead of interpolating runtime exception messages.
- No authorisation model anywhere in the product: one human role (the developer at the terminal), no admin, no multi-user model, no tenancy. Anyone who can run the process can read and change every setting. The only permission-like check in the product is the platform check gating the OS credential keystore.
- Secret-handling rule: secret values are never printed to any user-facing surface; they are shown only as '***set***' or '(not set)' accompanied by a source label. (The startup and post-store migration-instruction path violates this today and is recorded as a quirk.)
- Credential resolution ordering is product-wide: environment variable first (first non-empty in a per-secret ordered list), then the OS secret store only when the toggle is on (failures swallowed and treated as absent), then the deprecated in-document slot. Credentials are resolved on every read, so environment changes take effect without restart.
- Culture rule: parse and format all persisted numbers and all command input with a fixed invariant culture. Packaging or build configuration must never change input validation or number formatting.
- Observability convention: all diagnostics are plain text on standard output. There is no structured logging, no log level and no way to silence startup notices - and any diagnostic printed by a service is invisible when a windowed shell owns the screen.
- Interactive-prompt rule: no service may read standard input directly. Yes/no and numbered-choice confirmations must go through an injectable prompt/confirm port so that windowed and headless hosts can drive them.
- Command-line convention: neither entry point parses command-line flags or arguments. There are no profiles, no per-workspace configuration, no configuration layering and no runtime configuration-path override; environment variables affect credentials only.
- Persistence convention: state is persisted as an indented, hand-editable structured document written in full on every change (no dirty tracking, no explicit save verb, no partial patch, no merge, no backup, no atomic replace); hand edits are read back verbatim with no validation or repair, and a corrupt document is left untouched until the next successful save.
- Localisation: all user-visible strings are hard-coded English; there is no localisation layer anywhere in the product.
- Command dispatch and argument parsing: a line beginning with '/' is a command; the remainder is split on the space character with empty entries discarded. Runs of spaces collapse, tab characters are NOT separators, and there is NO quoting or escaping anywhere in the product. The command word and each command's subcommand token are lower-cased for dispatch; user-supplied names and file paths are case-preserved.
- Command result convention: every command invocation returns a triple of (succeeded?, message text, exit-requested?). Failure is carried in the flag plus a human-readable message; there is no error-code scheme anywhere in the product.
- Error message convention: all failure messages are plain, unlocalized English embedded in the code, with no structured logging and nothing written to a log file. Wrapped errors nest verbatim, e.g. 'Error processing prompt command: Error saving system prompt: <reason>'.
- File path convention: user-supplied paths are used exactly as typed - no home-directory ('~') expansion, no environment-variable expansion, and no directory creation - even though the user manual's own examples use '~/'-prefixed paths.
- Persistence root convention: the settings file lives under the user home directory at ~/.ChatDbg/settings.json (falling back to the OS temp directory if the home directory is unreadable), while the prompt library lives under the per-user LOCAL APPLICATION DATA directory at <local app data>/ChatDbg/. Two different roots, deliberately.
- Timestamp convention: all timestamps are stored in UTC and displayed converted to local time using the current culture's general short date-and-time pattern - date plus hours and minutes, no seconds.
- Collation convention: every user-visible list is sorted by culture-sensitive collation, not byte order. The product's size-optimized publish profiles disable globalization data, which changes both ordering and timestamp rendering for the same data.
- Security model: there is no authentication, no authorization, no multi-user model and no per-user scoping beyond the operating-system account. Any code path in the process may read, write or delete any stored artifact; protection is entirely the OS file-permission model on a per-user directory, created with the platform default mode with no explicit permissions set.
- Concurrency model: single-process, single-threaded assumptions throughout. Whole-file overwrite with no locking, no atomic temp-file-and-rename, and no optimistic-concurrency token; two instances running at once clobber each other and a crash mid-write leaves a truncated file.
- Cancellation: no operation anywhere accepts a cancellation signal and none is offered to the user, so a slow or hung operation cannot be aborted.
- Internationalization: all screen strings are hard-coded English with no resource lookup or message catalogue, while timestamps and ordering are culture-sensitive - so the product is unlocalized in text but locale-dependent in rendering.
- Usage-string convention: every usage and error string that means 'a name' uses the literal placeholder token '<n>', consistently across the code, the error messages and the user manual, even though it reads as 'a number'.
- Wiring convention: there is no dependency-injection container anywhere; every collaborator is constructed by hand in the shell's initialization. This is the direct cause of the full-screen shell's orphaned-settings defect and should be reviewed product-wide rather than per-feature.
- Uniform command contract: every user-facing command exposes a unique lower-case name, a one-line description, a multi-line usage text, and an asynchronous execute operation over an array of string arguments that yields a result carrying success, an optional message, and an exit-requested flag. Static factories produce success, error and exit results.
- Command input parsing: input beginning with '/' is a command, everything else is a chat turn. The shell strips the leading '/', splits the remainder on spaces discarding empty segments, lower-cases the first segment as the command name, and passes the remaining segments as arguments. Argument parsing beyond that is ad-hoc inside each command.
- Command result rendering: the console shell prefixes a success message with '✓ ' and a failure message with '✗ '; the full-screen shell shows a success message in the status bar and a failure message in a modal 'Command Error' dialog.
- Unknown-command message: the console shell prints exactly "Unknown command: /<name>. Type '/help' for available commands."; the full-screen shell raises an 'Unknown command' error dialog.
- No dependency-injection container exists anywhere in the product; every collaborator is constructed by hand at its owner's construction site. This is the direct cause of the export-logs command being unable to reach a live service instance.
- Per-user storage roots differ by state kind: diagnostic logs under the roaming application-data root, system prompts under the local application-data root, settings under the user-profile root. A clone should choose one convention and be told these three differ today.
- Every documented per-user file path in the product is written in Windows form only; the non-Windows renderings are documented nowhere despite the product describing itself as cross-platform.
- All user-visible text - command names, descriptions, usage strings, error and success messages, log level labels and log message text - is hard-coded English with no resource lookup and no localization hook.
- The product's own documentation set is unreliable and code wins on every disagreement: this feature alone found seven separate points where documentation contradicts code (unreachable command, non-functional export, unworkable export workflow, stale dependency version, non-existent configuration surface, half-true flush promise, self-defeating troubleshooting advice).
- Publish-configuration policy: the compact and single-file configurations force invariant globalization, substitute bare framework resource keys for exception messages, strip symbols, disable stack-trace metadata, suppress trim-analysis warnings, and default the runtime identifier to 64-bit Windows. These choices materially degrade every diagnostic surface in the product, not only this feature's.
- Diagnostic-channel policy: the platform debug-trace sink used throughout the product (both hosted providers write to it directly too) is removed at compile time in any build lacking the diagnostic-build symbol, which no configuration defines outside stock Debug. No subsystem should route its own internal failure reports through a channel that can vanish at build time.
- Failure-degradation convention for diagnostics: diagnostics must never break the thing they are diagnosing. Every operation in a diagnostic path swallows its own exceptions and returns no status. A clone should preserve the property but add an out-of-band health signal, since today a non-functional diagnostic subsystem is indistinguishable from a healthy one.
- Test-hygiene conventions observed: tests touch the real filesystem with no abstraction over file I/O, some write into the system temporary directory without cleaning up, and some carry a descriptive name with zero assertions in the body. A clone should place the clock, the file store and the debug sink behind seams and require at least one assertion per named behaviour.
- No authentication, authorization, tenancy, roles or audit exists anywhere in the product. The process runs entirely with the invoking operating-system user's privileges; the only gate is 'does the active provider have credentials', which is a capability check, not a security check.
- Recoverable-error convention: every failure prints a human-readable line and returns the user to the prompt; no handled error ever ends the session and no error ever removes anything from the transcript. Only a fault escaping the outermost run method is fatal.
- Fatal-error convention: print 'Fatal error: {message}' to standard output and exit with process status 1; normal termination exits with status 0. Disposal runs before the fatal message is printed.
- Command outcome convention: a uniform three-signal result (success, optional message, exit request); a non-empty message renders as '✓ {message}' on success and '✗ {message}' on failure in one write, so multi-line messages carry the marker on the first physical line only; an empty message renders nothing.
- Diagnostic convention: full fault detail (type and call stack) goes only to a platform debug channel, never to the user. There is no application log file for the shells themselves.
- File path conventions: user settings at <user profile>/.ChatDbg/settings.json (falling back to the system temporary directory when the profile is unresolvable); system prompts at <local app data>/ChatDbg/system_prompts/{sanitized name}.json with invalid filename characters replaced by underscore; diagnostic logs at <roaming app data>/ChatDbg/Logs/.
- Persistence convention: structured documents written indented with explicit, fixed key names (not derived from a naming convention); computed values and resolved secrets are never written. Existing user files must keep working, so key names are a compatibility contract.
- Timestamp convention: every persisted timestamp is coordinated universal time. The one exception - the local-model diagnostic log file name, which uses the local date - is a defect recorded as a quirk.
- Credential resolution priority is fixed product-wide: (1) environment variables in declared order, (2) the machine secret vault when explicitly enabled, (3) the deprecated value in the settings file. Values are never snapshotted; each read re-consults the chain, so a credential appearing mid-process takes effect immediately.
- Credential display convention: the product displays a credential's SOURCE LABEL, never its value - exactly one of 'environment variable ({VARIABLE_NAME})', 'Windows Credential Manager', 'settings file (deprecated)', 'not set'. (The settings-store migration path violates this and is recorded as a quirk.)
- The machine secret vault must be modelled as an optional, pluggable secret source with a working 'unavailable' branch; on hosts without it, all vault guidance is hidden and vault instructions refuse.
- No cancellation token, timeout, retry, idle expiry or interrupt handling exists anywhere in the product. Long or hung operations block until they finish or the process is killed.
- No internationalization or localization: every string is hard-coded US English embedded at the call site, with no resource files. Only number formatting follows the ambient culture, which is itself a source of build-configuration-dependent divergence.
- Setting validation is enforced only at the setting-instruction boundary, never when persisted values are read back. A reimplementer must decide product-wide whether to validate on load.
- Terminal output encoding is never configured anywhere; all non-ASCII glyphs assume a Unicode-capable host terminal.
- Settings file convention: all application settings live in a single indented, camel-cased UTF-8 JSON document at <user profile>/.ChatDbg/settings.json; the directory is created on demand, and the same file name is used in the operating-system temp directory when the profile directory is unavailable or resolves to an empty string.
- Settings persistence convention: every mutating command writes the ENTIRE settings document back immediately after mutation. Save failures are swallowed - the persistence layer prints 'Error saving settings: {message}' and returns normally, and the calling command still reports success, so the change survives only in memory.
- Settings load convention: a missing settings file causes a defaults file to be written at startup; a corrupt or unreadable file causes 'Error loading settings: {message}' to be printed and an all-defaults object to be returned, silently discarding the user's configuration.
- Command contract: user input beginning with '/' is a command; everything else is a conversation turn. Each command exposes a name, a description, a usage string and an asynchronous execute over an argument array, returning a result carrying success, an optional message and an exit-requested flag. Arguments are split on spaces with empty entries removed and the command word is lower-cased before dispatch.
- Error-reporting convention: user-facing failures are returned as a failure result with a plain-English message; exception detail is written only to a diagnostic trace channel that is invisible in release runs and never reaches the operator.
- Full-screen shell feedback convention: a successful command result appears on the transient status line prefixed with a check mark; a failure opens a modal titled 'Command Error'; a thrown exception opens a modal titled 'Error'. Any status message reverts to 'Provider: {p} | Model: {m} | Prompt: {n}' after 3 seconds, and because the revert timer is fire-and-forget, two messages within 3 seconds cause the first timer to clear the second early.
- Export/import convention: conversation exports are indented, camel-cased JSON written to a user-chosen path, and must be re-importable by the same product; members with an explicit persisted name (such as top_alternatives) keep that name and override the camel-case rule; derived, computed members are never exported.
- Security model: there is no authentication, no authorization, no per-user scoping and no server component anywhere in the product. The only security-adjacent behaviour is credential sourcing for provider access, which belongs to the credential-management capability.
- Concurrency and cancellation: the product is single-threaded and interactive; no operation anywhere supports cancellation. The only concurrency guard in the codebase is the local in-process provider's process-wide one-generation-at-a-time gate.
- Localization and formatting: all user-visible strings are hard-coded English with no localization mechanism. Numeric formatting follows the ambient locale, so decimal separators and percent spacing vary by machine unless invariant formatting is forced - and the optimized publish configurations do force it, so the same build produces different output depending on how it was published.
- Terminal capability assumptions: colour, box-drawing borders and the Unicode glyphs used for indicators, column headers and status prefixes assume a modern Unicode-capable terminal; no monochrome or ASCII-only fallback is ever selected at runtime even though an ASCII renderer exists in the codebase.
- Wiring convention: there is no dependency-injection container; every command is constructed by hand with its collaborators in each shell's initialization routine and stored in a dictionary keyed by command name. A clone must ensure all surfaces share ONE configuration instance, which the source's full-screen shell fails to do.
- Build and packaging convention: the optimized publish configurations hard-default to a Windows-x64 runtime identifier and enable trimming and ahead-of-time compilation, which breaks any late-bound or reflection-based code path; the pinned SDK file at the surveyed commit is malformed JSON and prevents the toolchain from running inside the tree at all.
- No authentication, authorization, role check, permission model, multi-tenancy or redaction exists anywhere in the product. Any message content, including anything a user pasted, is rendered verbatim to whoever is at the terminal. The only scoping is the operating-system account whose per-user directory holds the settings and prompt files.
- Per-user file path convention: all persisted product state lives under «user profile directory»/.ChatDbg/ (settings at settings.json), with a temporary directory used as the fallback when the user-profile directory cannot be resolved. The directory name is the literal '.ChatDbg'.
- No product behaviour reads any environment variable. There are none anywhere in the product's runtime configuration.
- Error-presentation convention, plain console host: a successful command result renders as '✓ «message»', a failure as '✗ «message»'; any unhandled loop failure renders as 'Error: «message»' with detail sent only to the platform debug channel, and the loop continues.
- Error-presentation convention, full-screen host: a successful command result is shown as a transient status message prefixed '✓ '; a failed command opens a modal dialog titled 'Command Error'; an unknown command opens a modal titled 'Error' with body 'Unknown command: «name»'; any other failure opens a modal titled 'Error' carrying the failure message, after which the transcript is still rebuilt.
- There is no logging framework anywhere in the product. Diagnostics go to the platform debug channel only, and are invisible to the user in a normal run.
- All user-visible strings are hard-coded English literals. There are no resource files, no localisation, and no string catalogue.
- There is no dependency-injection container. Concrete services are constructed by each host's start-up routine and hand-passed to the components that need them — which is what makes the split-settings defect (QUIRK-12.18) possible.
- Every mutation of a persisted setting writes the settings file immediately; there is no explicit save step and no dirty-tracking.
- Validation semantics differ deliberately by entry point: the command surface rejects out-of-range values with a message and leaves the stored value unchanged, while the graphical settings dialog silently clamps them into range with no message. A clone must decide whether to unify these.
- Source and document text encoding must be one consistent encoding (UTF-8). The source repository already contains two casualties — a pure-ASCII README whose rendered examples are '?'-mojibake, and a source file carrying a lone 0x95 Windows-1252 byte — so literals must be re-authored rather than copied.
- Packaged builds force invariant globalization in their compact and single-file configurations, which changes culture-sensitive number formatting (a space before the percent sign). Any clone must pin a culture explicitly rather than relying on the ambient one.
- Nothing in the presentation layer is cached: views are torn down and rebuilt widget-by-widget on every refresh, giving O(total lines) allocations per refresh for a long conversation.
- Process exit contract (product-wide): the application exits with code 0 on clean shutdown, and with code 1 after any unhandled failure, preceded by exactly one line 'Fatal error: <exception message>' on standard output. No stack trace and no log file are produced on that path. This is the only machine-readable signal the shipped program emits and it applies to both shells.
- Read-only install contract (product-wide): the application writes nothing into the directory it was launched from. Every piece of persistent state resolves through well-known per-user directory lookups at run time. Any feature that adds persistence must follow this or the single-file distribution unit stops working.
- Per-user state path conventions (product-wide): settings at <user profile>/.ChatDbg/settings.json with a silent fallback to <temp>/settings.json; system prompts at <local application data>/ChatDbg/system_prompts/; diagnostic logs at <application data>/ChatDbg/Logs. Three roots under two naming conventions, none documented, with no uninstall action.
- Environment-over-stored credential precedence (product-wide): credential values are read from environment variables in preference to stored values, and the reported credential source contains the phrase 'environment variable'. Recognised names: CHATDBG_AZURE_API_KEY, CHATDBG_AWS_ACCESS_KEY, and AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID (whose mere presence switches the cloud client to the ambient credential chain).
- Self-provisioning first run (product-wide): loading settings when no settings document exists returns a populated default object AND writes those defaults; constructing the prompt store creates its directory and seeds the default prompts. A freshly downloaded binary must start on a machine with no prior state and no shipped configuration file.
- Run-time rather than compile-time platform guards (product-wide): platform-specific capabilities are guarded by run-time operating-system checks, never by conditional compilation or per-platform source variants. Absent capabilities return 'no value' rather than throwing. This is what allows one unmodified source tree to be published to every supported platform with one build profile.
- Static analysability requirement (product-wide): any code reachable only by reflection, dynamic type discovery, or name-based lookup is at risk of removal in a shipped build, and analysis diagnostics are suppressed so violations surface only at run time. Every feature that persists or discovers anything must be written to be statically analysable, or the build must not trim.
- Shipped-build behaviour divergence (product-wide): shipped builds run culture-invariant with English-only resources, carry no debug information, and (in the native profile) substitute symbolic keys for framework message text and omit stack-trace metadata. Any feature relying on culture-sensitive comparison or formatting, or on error text matching what a developer saw, is affected.
- No accounts, no authorization, no per-user separation in the product itself: whoever runs the process owns all of its state. Packaging assumes exactly one user per user account.
- Version metadata is hand-maintained in multiple independent places with no reconciliation step; any product-wide versioning policy must name a single source of truth.
- Credential resolution order is product-wide: environment variable first, then the operating-system credential store only if the 'use credential store' flag is on, then the deprecated plaintext value in the settings file. The exact variable names are CHATDBG_AZURE_API_KEY; CHATDBG_AWS_ACCESS_KEY then AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID; CHATDBG_AWS_SECRET_KEY then AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY, and the credential-store target names are ChatDbg:AzureApiKey, ChatDbg:AwsAccessKey and ChatDbg:AwsSecretKey.
- File path conventions are product-wide: the settings file is <user profile>/.ChatDbg/settings.json, falling back to the operating-system temporary directory when the profile folder cannot be resolved; prompt records live at <local application data>/ChatDbg/system_prompts/<sanitised name>.json with every file-name-invalid character replaced by an underscore; a leading tilde-slash is expanded to the user profile directory by the conversation import and export commands only; a conversation export path with no extension gets .json appended.
- Command routing and parsing is product-wide: input beginning with a slash is a command and anything else is a chat turn; the leading slash is stripped, the remainder split on single spaces with empty pieces discarded, the first token lowercased as the name and argument case preserved.
- Command-result contract is product-wide: a success result carries a message and exit false; a failure result carries a message and success false; an exit result carries success true, exit true and a null message - which is why exiting never produces a success line in any shell.
- Error-surfacing convention differs by shell and should be stated once at product level: the console shell prefixes success and failure lines with a check and a cross glyph, while the full-screen shell uses a check-prefixed status line for success and a modal box for every failure, never a cross glyph.
- Persistence-failure convention is product-wide and currently unsafe: the settings storage layer catches every exception, writes a plain-console diagnostic and returns normally, so no caller can report a failed settings write; likewise every storage diagnostic goes to the raw console, which is invisible or destructive under a full-screen interface.
- Security posture is product-wide: no authentication, no authorization, no role separation, no audit trail and no redaction of secrets in transcripts or error dialogs. The process runs with the invoking account's privileges and touches only that account's directories; secrets are masked in exactly one input field.
- Asynchrony convention is product-wide: all service and command entry points are asynchronous and there are no cancellation tokens anywhere in the product, so no operation can be cancelled from any surface.
- Culture handling needs a product-wide decision: numeric parsing and percentage/timestamp formatting use the ambient culture, while the size-optimised build configurations pin invariant culture, so the same input behaves differently across build flavours.
- Localisation posture is product-wide: every user-visible string is a hard-coded English literal and there is no resource or translation surface anywhere.
- Command dispatch convention (product-wide): a typed line beginning with '/' is a command; the marker is stripped, the remainder split on spaces discarding empty entries, the first element lower-cased is the command name and the rest are the argument list. Command names are matched case-insensitively and registered by their own lowercase declared name. A consequence carried by every command: runs of whitespace in the user's line are normalised to a single space before the command sees them.
- Command contract (product-wide): every user-facing command exposes a stable lowercase name, a one-line description, a usage string, and an asynchronous execute taking an argument list and returning a success flag, an optional message and an exit-request flag. The plain console shell prints a check mark plus the message on success and a cross mark plus the message on failure.
- Unknown-command handling (product-wide, currently inconsistent): the plain console shell answers 'Unknown command: /{name}. Type '/help' for available commands.' while the full-screen shell pops an error dialog reading 'Unknown command: {name}' with no leading marker and no pointer to help. One wording should be chosen product-wide.
- Command registration (product-wide): there is no dependency-injection container anywhere in the product; every shell hand-wires an explicit command list with directly constructed collaborators. Commands not present in that list are unreachable regardless of being implemented.
- Settings access convention (product-wide): commands hold a reference to a single shared mutable settings object and read it at execution time, so changes take effect without a restart - but only if the host mutates that object rather than replacing it. Replacing it silently detaches every already-constructed command.
- Settings file path convention (product-wide): the settings document lives at '~/.ChatDbg/settings.json'; when the user-profile folder resolves to an empty string the base directory silently becomes the platform temp directory, converting per-user settings into per-boot settings.
- Settings validation convention (product-wide): value ranges are enforced only at the point a setting is written (alternatives count 1-20, maximum response length 1-8192, context window 512-32768). Readers never re-validate, so a hand-edited settings document can carry out-of-range values into any feature.
- Log directory convention (product-wide): diagnostic logs live in the platform application-data folder joined with 'ChatDbg' then 'Logs'; entries are formatted '[{yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss.fff}] [{level}] {message}' and roll over daily into files named 'llamasharp_{yyyyMMdd}.log'.
- Error-reporting convention (product-wide): failures inside a command are caught, written in full to a diagnostic trace channel as 'Error in {CommandName}: {exception}', and surfaced to the user as a single-line failure message. Service-layer failures are wrapped once with a contextual prefix and the inner cause preserved, so users routinely see double-prefixed messages.
- Silent-failure convention (product-wide, undesirable): all diagnostic-log file operations and the analysis-export writer swallow every failure into a debug or log channel and report success to the caller. A user whose target directory is unwritable sees no error and gets no file.
- No authentication, authorization, multi-tenancy or user accounts exist anywhere in the product. The only human role is the local interactive operator, and arbitrary local file paths are accepted with no sandboxing.
- No cancellation exists anywhere in the product's local-inference paths - a long model load, generation or output dump cannot be aborted.
